Context: LSAT U is an LSAT prep app. Its database of LR (Logical Reasoning) explanations was pulled read-only to C:\Users\Joshu\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os\d175f546-f515-4224-a5ed-1afb844b5971\scratchpad\golden-survey\db\ as four files:
- lr_questions.json: id, qid, preptest, section, question_number, question_type, stimulus, prompt, correct_choice
- lr_explanations.json: id, question_id, style
- lr_explanation_steps.json: explanation_id, position, label, body. These form the Argument Breakdown.
- lr_explanation_choice_notes.json: explanation_id, letter, body. These are the Choice Notes.

The database is the source of truth. Ignore the local Explanations/*.md files. For per-question difficulty and features, use public/data/PT*-LR.json in C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\projects\lsatu-wt-parser (read-only), matched on preptest, section and question number.

A previous survey defined what a strong explanation looks like. Read C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\corpus\lr-explanations\golden-set.md and golden-set-observations.md first; that rubric is your standard. Note that Joshua is fine with personal jabs at the test itself; do not flag those.

Goal: find the existing explanations that are weak enough to revamp, i.e. rewrite later in the golden-set voice. The PT157 and PT158 explicit "standard" style rows are already known to be bad: em-dash heavy, formulaic, factual slips. Include all of them, and confirm this with a sample.

Steps:
1. **Heuristic scan of every explanation** (3,891 rows; for questions with both a 'standard' and a 'single' row, use 'standard'). Flag:
   - missing or very short breakdown
   - fewer than 5 choice notes
   - verdict-only notes, under about 15 words or just "irrelevant" / "out of scope" with no reason
   - a correct-answer note that is thinner than the wrong-answer notes
   - no prediction or Goal step
   - em-dash density far above the corpus norm
   - a note that quotes text not found in the question's choices (possible misquote)
   - a note or step mentioning a letter different from correct_choice as right (possible key conflict)
   - obvious transcription damage, such as garbled diagrams, stray markup, or truncated text
   Compute a weakness score and save everything as inventory.csv.
2. **Read in full** the top ~150 by weakness score, plus a random 30 from the rest as a check on your heuristics, and at least 20 from PT157/158. Confirm or reject each flag by reading the actual text against the question.
3. **Write revamp-list.md.** Group it by severity:
   - **Revamp**: the explanation fails as teaching or has errors.
   - **Weak**: complete, but loose enough that a student gets little from it. Worth reading.
   - **Watch**: minor problems.
   For each item give: PT-S-Q, question type, difficulty, the problem categories (as tags), and one line describing the specific problem. Group within severity by question type. Give counts at the top.
4. **Write choice-note-issues.md** listing individual Choice Note problems you confirmed. Use a table: question, choice letter, problem, and a short quote where useful. Include misquotes, wrong logic, key conflicts and verdict-only notes on the popular trap.
5. **Write side-issues.md** listing anything else worth fixing that you notice. Examples: data or labelling problems, questions whose explanation seems to argue for a different answer than correct_choice (a possible mis-key), and missing stimulus text.

Return a concise summary, under 400 words: the counts per severity, the worst 10 with a one-line reason each, any suspected mis-keys, what your random check says about how accurate the heuristics are, and the file paths.

8	# LR golden set: the 51 explanations new ones are modelled on
9	
10	The model set for writing the 772 missing LR explanations (plan: [[../../brainstorms/2026-09-26-lr-explanation-coverage]]). Every pick is an existing LSAT Lab explanation in the LSAT U database (the database, not the local markdown, is the source of truth), read in full and scored against a rubric built by comparing strong and loose explanations. They are references, not edits: nothing here changes an existing explanation.
11	
12	- Full rubric, strong-vs-loose comparison, step structure per type, voice notes: [[golden-set-observations]]
13	- All 80 candidates, scores and the family x feature coverage grid: [[golden-set-candidates]]
14	
15	## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one
16	
17	Length does not. These do:
18	
19	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.
20	2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.
21	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").
22	4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**
23	5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.
24	
25	Complete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.
26

1	# Observations: LR Standard explanations
2	
3	Survey date: 2026-09-26. Read-only. Primary corpus: the DB dump in `db/` (PT101 to PT159). The local markdown in `lsatu-wt-parser/Explanations/` (PT139 to PT159) was used to check layout and choice tags, which the dump does not include.
4	
5	## 1. What the corpus actually is
6	
7	**Styles.** `lr_explanations.style` has four values: `single` 3,183, `standard` 236, `brief` 236, `another_way` 236. Every question with a `standard` row also has `brief` and `another_way`, and no question has both `standard` and `single`. So "the Standard explanation" is `standard` where it exists and `single` everywhere else. Both have the same shape (Argument breakdown steps, then five choice notes). 352 of 3,771 questions have no explanation at all (`is_explained = false`).
8	
9	**Where the explicit Standard lives.** 126 of the 236 `standard` rows are PT157 and PT158. The rest are scattered one to nine per test (PT101-110, PT117-133, PT137-144, PT148, PT156).
10	
11	**Totals per family** (3,419 explained questions; "complete" = full breakdown plus five choice notes; "with prediction" = also has a Goal / Anticipate / Predict step; E/M/H = difficulty 1-2 / 3 / 4-5 among complete):

26	| Agree/Disagree | 119 | 7 | 112 | 112 | 97 | 47 | 26 | 35 |
27	| Parallel | 100 | 6 | 94 | 97 | 78 | 26 | 31 | 36 |
28	| Principle-Conform (apply / illustrate) | 99 | 8 | 91 | 85 | 71 | 41 | 14 | 26 |
29	| Method | 91 | 13 | 78 | 85 | 84 | 37 | 21 | 25 |
30	| Must Be False | 43 | 2 | 41 | 40 | 39 | 9 | 4 | 25 |
31	| Evaluate | 28 | 4 | 24 | 27 | 27 | 11 | 6 | 8 |
32	| Fill-in / Complete | 9 | 0 | 9 | 7 | 7 | 4 | 0 | 3 |
33	
34	Family comes from the question stem checked against the DB `question_type` label: they agree on 2,961 of 3,419 rows (87%); the rest are resolved by the label (379) or by the bank type where it agrees with the stem (79). I did not trust the bank's `questionType`: in the PT139-159 overlap it disagrees with the file label on 224 of 1,234 questions, and many of those are a default "Most Strongly Supported" on questions that are plainly Flaw, Paradox or Role.
35	
36	**Data problems found (not fixed, reported only).**
37	- The local `Explanations/PT140-explanations.md` is a thin, different version: about 100 words per question against about 630 in the DB. All 69 PT140 overlaps differ; every other PT139-159 question matches the DB within 20 words. Anyone reading PT140 from the markdown sees the wrong text.
38	- Bank alignment: 110 explained DB questions have no matching bank entry at the same PT/section/question, and 27 match a bank entry filed under another number (for example, the local PT148-S4-Q24 to Q26 stimuli sit in the bank under PT149-S4-Q24 to Q26). Features and difficulty for those are missing or borrowed; the inventory column `bank_match` says which.
39	- Transcription damage recurs in diagrams: arrow diagrams flattened onto one line (PT139-S4-Q8, PT139-S4-Q21), and in PT144-S2-Q22 the negation marks were lost so two diagrams now read backwards.
40	- Leftover source references: "try this one" pointing at a missing link (PT139-S1-Q5), "Welcome to Test 89" (PT155-S4-Q15).
41	- The bank carries a "Games-y LR" feature tag (for example PT119-S4-Q24). Worth knowing given the no-Logic-Games rule for student copy.
42	
43	## 2. Quality by test range
44	
45	| PTs | Explained | Avg words | Complete | Em-dashes per 1k words | Notes |
46	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
47	| 101-122 | 1,158 | 553 | 85% | 0.5 | Full LSAT Lab voice. Many golden picks come from here. |
48	| 123-138 | 1,025 | 362 | 51% | 0.7 | Noticeably thinner. Most "incomplete" rows have a breakdown but no Goal step (346), and choice notes are often one or two lines. Some notes read like generic summaries (PT126-S4-Q20: "This introduces arbitrators, which are outside the scope"). |
49	| 139-156 | 1,097 | 588 | 91% | 0.6 | Same voice as 101-122. Consistently strong. |
50	| 157-159 | 139 | 672 | 89% | 7.1 | PT157/158 are explicit Standard. Breakdowns are in the house voice; choice notes are a different register (below). |
51	
52	**The PT157/158 explicit-Standard register.** Choice notes run about 100+ words each, open with formulas ("This answer captures...", "This answer identifies the argument's central flaw"), use em-dashes at roughly ten times the rate of everything else, and repeat the same point several ways. More importantly, they contain accuracy slips I did not find in the single-style text at the same rate:
53	- PT158-S3-Q2: the D note describes a different answer ("can effectively combine knowledge") from the actual choice ("can effectively solve many problems"); A is tagged "No Impact" while its note argues it undermines the argument.
54	- PT158-S2-Q16: the correct-answer note imports outside history (Plato's family wealth, Philip of Macedon) to justify the answer.
55	- PT158-S2-Q18: the A note argues against a claim the choice does not make.
56	
57	That is why no explicit-Standard item made the golden set, and why the house voice rules (no em-dashes) matter for the writing guide.
58	
59	**Two voices inside the single style.** Most text is one conversational author (voice A: "Hey, author", "Let's look for", FABS, Curious Fact, Given that / How can it be that). A second, more formal author appears in about 15% of questions (voice B: "Beware of answers that...", "If you chose this answer...", "This answer choice..."). Voice B is accurate and structured but lighter on prediction and analogy. Examples: PT146-S2-Q8, PT151-S2-Q1, PT152-S4-Q5, PT154-S1-Q10, PT155-S4-Q1. Both voices can produce a strong explanation; voice A produces most of the best ones.
60	
61	## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)
62	
63	I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.
64	
65	- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
66	- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").
67	
68	Measured averages (strong vs loose):
69	
70	| Measure | Strong | Loose |
71	|---|---|---|
72	| Total words | 773 | 394 |
73	| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
74	| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
75	| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
76	| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
77	| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
78	| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
79	| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
80	| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
81	| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |
82	
83	What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:
84	
85	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
86	2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
87	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
88	4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
89	5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
90	6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.
91	
92	What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).
93	
94	## 4. Rubric used for the shortlist
95	
96	Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.
97	
98	Scored 0 / 1 / 2:
99	
100	| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
101	|---|---|---|---|
102	| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
103	| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
104	| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
105	| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
106	| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
107	| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |
108	
109	Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.
110	
111	Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).
112	
113	## 5. Step structure per family (DB, most common sequences)
114	
115	Labels normalized (parenthetical indicator words dropped: "Conclusion (therefore)" counts as Conclusion).
116	
117	- **Flaw (524):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (67%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (9%); with an Intermediate Conclusion step (5%). Evaluate usually names the famous flaw or builds the objection; Goal often gives both the formal and the "fails to consider" phrasing.
118	- **Must Be True / Most Supported (462):** Statements > Evaluate > Goal (45%); Statements only (16%). Statements is often a diagram. Evaluate scans for conditional, causal, math and pivot wording and names the inference to make.
119	- **Necessary Assumption (352):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (66%). Goal typically offers a linking answer and a defender answer, and says "negate it".
120	- **Weaken (335) / Strengthen (277):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (59% / 55%). Evaluate uses "Given that ... how could we argue that ..." or Curious Fact / Alternate Explanation / Plausibility.
121	- **Paradox (213):** "Apparent Paradox > Evaluate" (22%, older tests) or "Given that ... > How can it be that / Why is it that ... > Evaluate > Goal" (about 30% combined, often with Evaluate / Goal merged).
122	- **Sufficient Assumption (193):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (53%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (21%). Goal usually diagrams the missing link as the logic path.
123	- **Principle-Strengthen (181):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (48%). Goal is "If evidence, then conclusion" in general terms.
124	- **Principle-Conform (99):** mixed. Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (24%) when the principle is in the answers; Principle > Evaluate > Goal (9%) or Principle > Goal (7%) when it is in the stimulus.
125	- **Main Conclusion (144) / Role (126):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (42% / 53%), with an Intermediate Conclusion step in about 8%. Evaluate almost always opens with the reminder that the conclusion usually comes early (first sentence or after a pivot).
126	- **Parallel (100) / Parallel Flaw (123):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (49% / 67%), Evidence first in about 19-35%. Goal is an algebraic recipe (P1, P2, C) or a plain-language template.
127	- **Agree/Disagree (119):** speaker-named steps. "Speaker's Claims > Speaker's Responses > Goal", often a numbered claim list with "Does [speaker 2] disagree?" per claim.
128	- **Method (91):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (37%) for one speaker; "X's Conclusion > X's Evidence > Y's Response > Evaluate > Goal" for two speakers.
129	- **Evaluate (28):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (75%).
130	
131	## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions
132	
133	- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
134	- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
135	- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
136	- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
137	- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.
138	
139	## 7. `Explanations/targeted-questions/`
140	
141	Three files: a README and two explanations, PT134-S1-Q16 (Flaw, belief vs implication, answer A) and PT158-S4-Q24 (Weaken, food-supply chain, answer D). The README says they were "added for LSAT U use when Joshua needs specific questions available outside a full PrepTest-section explanation file."
142	
143	They are not LSAT Lab transcriptions. The layout is different and the parser will not read it: an H1 title, a metadata block (type, answer, LSAT U order, bank file), H2 sections for Stimulus, Prompt, Argument breakdown, **Written explanation** (a free prose section the LSAT Lab files do not have), Answer choice explanations with an italic tag, and a closing **Student takeaway**. The prose is written in LSAT U's own voice: no em-dashes, short sentences, jokes built on the stimulus ("the drug sneaks into the food supply wearing a tiny trench coat", "Still a raccoon in the kitchen"), and a one-line takeaway ("Break the link that matters. That's the move.").
144	
145	Authorship: git attributes them to `vnjkoxpl-42905` (Joshua's account) inside a bulk commit dated 2026-09-05 titled "Update RRC launch page copy", with no co-author line and no other reference in the repo or wiki. The style, the house-voice compliance and the README wording point to an AI assistant (Claude or Codex) drafting them for Joshua. That is an inference; the git history cannot confirm who typed them.
146	
147	They are useful as a picture of the target voice (especially "Written explanation" and "Student takeaway", which have no LSAT Lab equivalent). They are not in the golden set because they are outside the corpus and use a different structure.
148	
149	## 8. Caveats
150	
151	- All scores are one reader's judgment, formed by reading each candidate once. Treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.
152	- Features and difficulty come from the question bank. 110 explained questions have no bank match, and some shortlisted items have no features in the bank at all (PT143-S1-Q6, PT154-S1-Q10), so the coverage grid undercounts them.
153	- I read about 125 explanations in full or near-full, weighted toward the highest-scoring candidates in each family and difficulty band. I did not read all 3,000, so a better exemplar for a thin cell may exist.
154

236	PROMPT: Suppose there were some vegetables that were grown in soil to which fertilizer had never been applied. On the basis of the passage, which one of the following would have to be true regarding those vegetables?

238	  (B) It is not possible that the vegetables were grown in soil in which crops had been rotated.
239	  (C*) The vegetables were grown in soil that had not been completely depleted of nutrients but not necessarily soil in which crops had been rotated.
240	  (D) Whatever the health risks to humans from eating the vegetables, these risks would not be attributable to the use of pesticides on them.

119	STIM: Ecologist: Before finding a mate, male starlings decorate their nests with fragments of aromatic plants rich in compounds known to kill parasitic insects. Since these parasites are potentially harmful to nestlings, some researchers have hypothesized that the function of these decorations is nestling protection. However, males cease to incorporate such greenery once egg laying starts, which suggests instead that the function of the decorations is to attract females.
120	PROMPT: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the support for the ecologist's conclusion?
121	  (A) Adult starlings are able to defend themselves against parasitic insects.
122	  (B) Male starlings do not decorate their nests in areas with unusually small populations of parasitic insects.
123	  (C) Nestlings grow faster in nests that incorporate aromatic plants than in nests that do not.
124	  (D*) Male starlings tend to decorate their nests with a greater number of aromatic plants when a caged female is positioned adjacent to the nest.
125	  (E) The compounds in the aromatic plants used by the male starlings to decorate their nests are harmless to nestlings.
126	> A: This answer is trying to trick us into thinking it attacks the Opposing Idea, but really it has no impact. The fact that adults can defend themselves against parasites doesn't make it any less plausible that the decorations are to defend the babies against parasites.
127	> B: This is a "no cause, no effect" style strengthener for the Opposing Conclusion that the parasites are the cause of the decorating. If, in the absence of the cause (parasites), there is no effect (decorating), that strengthens the idea that the decorations are to ward off parasites.
128	> C: We're trying to support the idea that the reason we decorate is to attract the females. If the babies grow faster in decorated nests, this makes it less plausible that the decoration is about attracting the ladies and more plausible that it's about helping the babies in some way. It doesn't go so far as to explicitly support the Opposing Idea because the decorations could help the babies in a way that isn't about parasites, but nonetheless, if anything, this weakens our argument instead of strengthening it.
129	> D*: This makes the "attract a female" explanation more plausible with a classic "more cause, more effect" strengthener. While that might not seem to be that helpful at first glance, consider that the only evidence we had for this explanation to begin with was that males stopped decorating after the eggs were laid. Knowing there's at least a correlation between the presence of a female and this decorating behavior makes that explanation more plausible.
130	> E: This only tells us that, whatever reason the male starlings have for using these plants, they aren't harming the nestlings in the process. This doesn't support or undermine either of the possible explanations for the behavior.

Because this answer is at least suggestive of an alternate explanation that would hurt the author's argument (population increase in preserve is just from other parts of the valley), this could be a correct answer on a Weaken question. 3	STIM: Art student: Great works of art evoke passionate responses in those who view them. Thus, since it is well known in art circles that the provocative work of abstract painter Ezekiel Reilly elicits intensely emotional responses in those who view them, his art is great.
4	PROMPT: Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the argument’s reasoning?
5	  (A) One of the premises used to support the argument’s conclusion assumes the truth of that conclusion.
6	  (B*) The argument treats a condition that is necessary for having a certain quality as if it must also be sufficient for having that quality.
7	  (C) The argument misapplies a general claim about the members of a class to an atypical instance of that class.
8	  (D) The argument contains a generalization derived from an insufficient number of instances.
9	  (E) The argument draws a conclusion expressing a value judgment from premises that do not involve statements about values.
10	- [Evidence] Great works of art evoke passionate responses in those who view them. (GW → PR) The work of abstract painter Ezekiel Reilly elicits intensely emotional responses in those who view them. (PR)
11	- [Conclusion] His art is great. (GW)
12	- [Evaluate] In order to draw this conclusion, we must assume that any work of art which elicits a passionate response is a great work. This is an illegal reversal of the first premise. (PR → GW)
13	> A: Circular reasoning occurs when a premise is essentially a restatement of the conclusion — the argument assumes what it is trying to prove. That is not happening here. The premise that great art evokes passionate responses is not the same as the conclusion that Reilly's art is great. The premise about Reilly's work evoking emotional responses is also not a restatement of the conclusion. These are distinct claims. The argument's problem is that it reverses the conditional, not that it assumes its own conclusion.
14	> B*: This precisely describes the argument's flaw. The first premise establishes that evoking passionate responses is a necessary condition for being great art — all great art has this quality. The argument then treats this necessary condition as if it were also sufficient — as if having this quality guarantees greatness. But many things that are not great art could evoke passionate responses. Provocative, disturbing, or even terrible art can elicit intense emotions. The argument assumes that because Reilly's art satisfies a necessary condition for greatness, it must therefore be great. This is the formal fallacy of affirming the consequent, equivalently described as confusing necessary and sufficient conditions.
15	> C: This answer suggests the argument misapplies a general claim to an atypical instance. But there is no reason to believe Reilly's work is atypical of the class being discussed. The argument applies the claim "great art evokes passion" to Reilly's work, which does evoke passion. The problem is not that Reilly's work is an unusual case that does not fit the general claim — it is that the argument reverses the direction of the conditional. Even if Reilly's work were perfectly typical, the argument would still be flawed.
16	> D: This answer describes a hasty generalization — drawing a broad conclusion from too few instances. The argument is not making a generalization at all. It is making a specific claim about one artist's work (Reilly's art is great). There is no indication that the argument uses an insufficient number of Reilly's works or an insufficient number of viewer reactions. The flaw is structural — reversing a conditional — not statistical.
17	> E: This answer claims the argument draws a value judgment from non-value premises. But the first premise is itself a value statement: "great works of art evoke passionate responses" contains the value-laden term "great." The conclusion ("his art is great") and the premise both involve the concept of artistic quality. So the argument does not lack value-laden premises — it has one. The flaw is not about moving from factual premises to value conclusions; it is about reversing the direction of a conditional relationship within those value claims.

42	  (D*) A hardware retail company wants to construct a new, larger warehouse and wants its employees to help plan how the old warehouse will be expanded to meet any need for more space.

44	- [Situation] You want your PT patient to better enjoy the challenge of developing physical skills. You also want them to spend more time practicing those physical skills.

The analysis pattern applies: if students understand the principles being taught (objective 1), they will naturally enjoy the learning experience more (objective 2). Understanding creates engagement, which creates enjoyment. This mirrors the original: achieving a cognitive/experiential improvement (understanding/enjoyment) leads to a behavioral outcome (enjoyment/more time). The causal link between comprehension and enjoyment is a standard motivational connection — people tend to enjoy activities they understand and can succeed at. This fits the pattern.
48	> B: The software manufacturer wants customers to (1) be more satisfied with its product and (2) have customers recommend the product to others. The analysis pattern applies: if customers are more satisfied (objective 1), they will naturally recommend the product to others (objective 2). Satisfied customers become advocates — this is the core logic of word-of-mouth marketing. The causal link is direct and intuitive: positive experience leads to positive recommendation. This perfectly mirrors the original's pattern where an internal state improvement (satisfaction/enjoyment) leads to a desired behavioral outcome (recommendations/more practice time).
49	> C: The librarian wants (1) fewer borrowed books to be lost or stolen and (2) borrowers to return books in good condition. The analysis pattern applies: if fewer books are lost or stolen (objective 1), borrowers are treating books more responsibly overall, which means they will also return books in better condition (objective 2). Both objectives are symptoms of the same underlying behavior — responsible handling of borrowed materials. Fostering the care that prevents loss and theft (objective 1) naturally extends to the care that preserves book condition (objective 2). The causal mechanism is about a general improvement in responsibility that manifests in multiple positive outcomes.
50	> D*: The hardware company wants (1) to construct a new, larger warehouse and (2) its employees to start working on Saturdays. These two objectives have no natural causal connection. Building a bigger warehouse does not cause employees to be willing to work Saturdays. Unlike every other answer, where achieving the first objective creates the conditions for the second, these two goals are completely independent. A new warehouse is a physical infrastructure project; Saturday work is a labor scheduling decision. There is no mechanism by which constructing a building leads to employees agreeing to work an extra day. The company would need separate strategies for each goal. This breaks the pattern where "success in meeting the first objective brings about success in meeting the second."
51	> E: The concert series director wants (1) to present a more varied repertoire and (2) to attract new audience members. The analysis pattern applies: if the concert series offers more varied programming (objective 1), it will naturally attract new audience members who are drawn to the expanded offerings (objective 2). Variety broadens appeal — programming that previously only attracted fans of one genre now attracts fans of multiple genres. The causal link is direct: diversified content attracts a diversified audience. This mirrors the original pattern where an improvement in the offering (variety/enjoyment) leads to a desired participation outcome (new audience/more practice time).

54	STIM: Historian: Because medieval epistemology (theory of knowledge) is a complex subject, intellectual historians have, until recently, failed to produce a definition that would help to determine what should and what should not be included in it. Clearly, the solution is to define medieval epistemology simply as "the epistemological beliefs of the medieval epistemologists." That way, if we want to know whether medieval epistemology includes some epistemological claim, we just ask whether any medieval epistemologists believed it. If any did, it is part of medieval epistemology; if any medieval epistemologists believed the opposite, then that opposite claim is part of medieval epistemology.
55	PROMPT: Which one of the following, if true, would most weaken the historian's argument?
56	  (A) Medieval epistemologists held some of the same epistemological beliefs as did ancient epistemologists.
57	  (B) The epistemological beliefs of medieval epistemologists depended upon their beliefs about nonepistemological matters.
58	  (C) The writings of most medieval epistemologists include passages that are clearly not about epistemology.
59	  (D) Some medieval epistemologists had epistemological beliefs that contradicted the epistemological beliefs of other medieval epistemologists.
60	  (E*) There is much debate as to which medieval thinkers, if any, were epistemologists.
61	- [Conclusion] The solution for defining which things were believed by medieval epistemologists is to say it's "the epistemological beliefs of the medieval epistemologists".
62	- [Evidence] If any medieval epistemologists believed claim X, then we'll say "medieval epistemology believed X".
63	If any medieval epistemologists believed claim X was false, then we'll say "medieval epistemology did not believe X".
64	- [Evaluate] Won't this method lead to a bunch of contradictions, assuming that different medieval epistemologists probably believed different things.
65	If the 10 of them believed X was true and 10 of them believed the opposite were true, then following this author's plan we would say that "medieval epistemology both believed and didn't believe X".
66	- [Goal] Look for something pointing out this potential for self-contradictory statements about what medieval epistemology believed.
67	Or challenge the author's assumption that medieval epistemologists would have had unanimous consensus on beliefs, since that is the only way his plan would actually work.
68	> A: Whether medieval epistemologists shared beliefs with ancient epistemologists concerns the originality of their ideas, not the applicability of the proposed definition. Even if every belief they held was borrowed from antiquity, the definition still functions — you would identify the medieval epistemologists, catalog their beliefs, and end up with a medieval epistemology that mirrors the ancient version. Overlapping content between eras does not make the definition impossible to apply. This answer addresses the content of medieval epistemology, not the workability of the definition itself.
69	> B: This answer reveals that medieval epistemologists' beliefs were shaped by non-epistemological factors such as politics and religion. While historically illuminating, this does not challenge the proposed definition. The definition instructs us to identify medieval epistemologists and catalog their epistemological beliefs. The causes of those beliefs are irrelevant to this process. Even if every position they held was motivated by religious dogma rather than pure philosophical inquiry, those positions are still their epistemological beliefs. Knowing why they believed what they believed does not prevent us from identifying what they believed.
70	> C: This answer points out that medieval epistemologists wrote about topics beyond epistemology, meaning their works contain mixed content. The implication is that applying the definition would require separating epistemological from non-epistemological material. But this is a manageable editorial task, not a fatal flaw. Scholars regularly distinguish between a thinker's epistemological claims and their ethical, metaphysical, or theological writings. The definition does not require that medieval epistemologists wrote only about epistemology — just that their epistemological beliefs can be identified within their broader work. The truly devastating weakness would be one showing the definition is logically impossible to apply, not merely that it requires careful reading.
71	> D: If medieval epistemologists held contradictory beliefs, the proposed definition would simply produce a body of thought containing internal disagreements. But this is entirely normal for any intellectual tradition. Modern epistemology, ethics, physics — every discipline contains competing viewpoints among its practitioners. The definition explicitly accounts for this: if one epistemologist believed X and another believed the opposite, both are included. Internal contradiction is a feature of the definition, not a bug. This answer describes a consequence the historian already anticipated and accepted, not a problem that undermines the definition's applicability.
72	> E*: This answer exposes the definition's fatal circularity. The definition says: medieval epistemology consists of the beliefs held by medieval epistemologists. To apply it, you must first identify who the medieval epistemologists were. But this answer reveals that there is significant scholarly debate about which medieval thinkers qualify as epistemologists. Resolving that debate requires a clear understanding of what medieval epistemology encompasses — the very thing the definition is supposed to provide. The result is a vicious circle: you need the definition's output (what medieval epistemology is) to generate its input (who the medieval epistemologists were). Without independent criteria for identifying medieval epistemologists, the definition cannot get off the ground.

118	Main conclusions are the claims arguments try to establish, not the facts they take for granted. Background facts serve as premises; the conclusion is the contested claim built on top of them. Here, the warming data is the foundation, and the "far from justified" judgment is the building erected upon it. The argument is about whether the warming justifies a particular interpretation (that it demonstrates global warming), not about whether warming occurred.
119	> B: The climatologist does not flatly deny that the warming is a symptom of global warming. The climatologist's actual position is that the conclusion linking warming to global warming is "far from justified" — a claim about evidential sufficiency, not an outright denial. These are fundamentally different positions. "Far from justified" means the evidence is inadequate to support the global warming interpretation. It leaves open the possibility that global warming could be the cause; the argument merely contends that the evidence presented so far does not establish it. This mirrors the legal distinction between "not proven" and "proven false." The climatologist occupies the former position; this answer choice ascribes the latter. By upgrading cautious skepticism to categorical denial, this answer overstates the climatologist's claim.
120	> C*: The climatologist's argument contains several components: the acknowledged fact of ocean warming, the existence of natural temperature cycles, and the evaluative judgment that the global warming conclusion is "far from justified." The main conclusion is this last piece — the overarching claim that every other statement serves to establish. The argument's flow runs: natural ocean cycles could explain the warming (supporting premise), therefore the conclusion that warming is a symptom of global warming is far from justified (main conclusion). The warming data is a premise everyone accepts. The natural cycles point is the evidence. The "far from justified" judgment is the destination. To identify the main conclusion, ask: which claim is everything else aimed at establishing? 121	> D: The possibility that the observed warming is the result of a natural cycle is a supporting premise in the climatologist's argument. The direction of support runs from this claim toward the main conclusion, not the other way around.

So highly profitable corporations can save money by giving their employees expensive bonuses.
130	PROMPT: The argument requires the assumption that
131	  (A) only a few corporations give their employees bonuses that provide strong enough incentives for the employees to keep working hard even when they are not being monitored
132	  (B) if a highly profitable corporation could save money by giving its employees expensive bonuses, it is because giving such bonuses would reduce the amount of time its managers must spend monitoring those employees
133	  (C) the more valuable the managers’ time is at a corporation, the less likely it is that the corporation will actually have those managers spend time monitoring employees
134	  (D*) for people who are employees of highly profitable corporations where monitoring is reduced, expensive bonuses constitute strong incentives to keep working hard
135	  (E) a highly profitable corporation can save money by reducing its managers’ monitoring of employees only if its employees are given expensive bonuses
136	- [Evidence] Highly profitable corporations can save money by reducing the monitoring done by managers, as long as the employees are given strong incentives to keep working hard.
137	- [Conclusion] Highly profitable corporations can save money by giving their employees expensive bonuses.
138	- [Evaluate] To reach this conclusion about the employees at these particular corporations, we need to assume that there is a relationship between giving them strong incentives to keep working hard and giving them expensive bonuses. We have to assume that these bonuses would motivate them to keep working hard even when the managers aren't watching them closely.
139	> A: This answer says only a few corporations currently give bonuses that provide strong enough incentives. The argument is about what highly profitable corporations could do to save money, not about what corporations currently do. Whether many or few corporations already use effective bonuses does not affect whether the strategy would work if implemented. The argument makes a prescriptive claim about a potential policy, and the current adoption rate of that policy is irrelevant to its potential effectiveness. Additionally, this answer does not specify whether the bonuses are expensive or whether the corporations are highly profitable.
140	> B: This answer says if a corporation can save money by giving bonuses, it is because bonuses reduce monitoring time. Diagrammed: saves money via bonuses -> reduces monitoring. This uses the conclusion as the trigger (sufficient condition) and provides an explanation for why it works. But the argument needs to establish that the conclusion is true, not explain why it would be true if it were. A necessary assumption should be something that, if false, would destroy the argument. This answer, if negated ("it is not necessarily because of reduced monitoring"), does not destroy the argument — the argument has already established the monitoring-reduction mechanism in its premises.
141	> C: This answer says the more valuable managers' time is, the less likely the corporation will have managers monitor employees. At highly profitable corporations (where managers' time is most valuable), this suggests monitoring is already minimal. If monitoring is already low, there is little monitoring to reduce, which means less potential savings from the bonus strategy. This weakens rather than supports the argument. The argument depends on highly profitable corporations currently spending substantial manager time on monitoring, so there is something to save by reducing it. An answer suggesting they already do not monitor much undermines the premise that monitoring savings are available.
142	> D*: This answer states that for employees of highly profitable corporations where monitoring is reduced, expensive bonuses constitute strong incentives to keep working hard. This is the precise bridge the argument needs. The evidence says corporations can save money by reducing monitoring if employees get strong incentives. The conclusion says they can save money by giving expensive bonuses. This assumption connects the two by establishing that expensive bonuses are strong incentives for the relevant employees. Negation test: if expensive bonuses do not constitute strong incentives for these employees, then giving bonuses would not replace the need for monitoring, and no money would be saved. The argument collapses.
143	> E: This answer says corporations can save money by reducing monitoring only if employees receive expensive bonuses. The word "only" makes expensive bonuses the sole method for saving money through reduced monitoring. The argument only needs bonuses to be one effective method, not the only method. Maybe strong incentives could also come from stock options, promotions, or other rewards. The argument does not need to assume bonuses are the exclusive solution — it only needs to assume they are an effective one. Negation test: "Corporations can also save money through reduced monitoring using incentives other than expensive bonuses." This does not destroy the argument because bonuses can still work even if alternatives also work.

146	STIM: To test the claim that vitamin C is effective in treating acne, scientists administered it to one group of subjects and a placebo to a control group. The group receiving vitamin C had less severe acne during the study than did the control group. It was subsequently discovered, however, that half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell, no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups. Therefore, we can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.
147	PROMPT: Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument?
148	  (A*) The subjects who were given vitamin C had a history of suffering from more severe acne than did the subjects receiving a placebo.
149	  (B) None of the subjects who were given vitamin C took additional doses of vitamin C on their own.
150	  (C) During the study, the severity of the subjects’ acne was lower than the national average.
151	  (D) Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.
152	  (E) Some of the subjects who knew their pills were placebos did not actually take the pills they were given.
153	- [Opposing Evidence] During a study, a group of subjects receiving vitamin C had less severe acne than did the control group, which received a placebo.
154	- [Evidence] Half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell [which pill they were given], no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups.
155	- [Conclusion] We can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.
156	- [Evaluate] Let's make sure that we're clear about what happened to the different groups that are compared in this study. We know that some subjects who received vitamin C had less severe acne than those who received a placebo. Among the subjects who didn't know which kind of pill they were given, there was no difference in the severity of acne. This means that the subjects who received vitamin C and also had less severe acne all knew that they were taking vitamin C, not a placebo. At first, the study seems to show a correlation between taking vitamin C and a reduction in acne. That's evidence—not proof, but evidence—that vitamin C reduces acne. But wait! There's a problem with the study. If people knew which pill they were taking, that could affect the results somehow. From this the argument concludes—at least tentatively—that vitamin C does not help reduce the severity of acne. This looks like an "unproven vs. proven false" flaw. Because this one particular study had a problem, it hasn't necessarily proven that vitamin C helps treat acne. But it's still possible that vitamin C does help treat acne, even if this one study hasn't proven that it does. We can't jump from the details of this one study to a general claim about the benefits of vitamin C.
157	- [Goal] A correct answer might provide evidence that the vitamin C actually did help to reduce subjects' acne, in spite of the apparent problem with the way the study was conducted.
158	> A*: If the vitamin C subjects started with more severe acne than the placebo subjects, then the study results look very different. The vitamin C group had less severe acne during the study despite starting with worse acne — suggesting vitamin C actually reduced their acne enough to surpass the control group. Even among those who did not know which pill they received, having "no difference" in severity is significant if the vitamin C subgroup began with worse acne. Equal outcomes from unequal starting points suggest the treatment was effective. This undermines the conclusion that vitamin C provides no real benefit.
159	> B: This answer rules out a potential confounding factor — vitamin C subjects secretly taking extra vitamin C. But ruling out this possibility does not change the study's findings or the argument's reasoning. The study already showed the vitamin C group had less severe acne overall but no difference among subjects who did not know their pill. Whether anyone took extra doses does not affect this comparison. Even if they did take extra, we would need to know whether those subjects were in the knowing or unknowing subgroup to evaluate the impact.
160	> C: Whether all subjects had milder acne than the national average is irrelevant to the argument. The argument compares groups within the study — vitamin C versus placebo, knowing versus unknowing. External comparisons to the national average do not affect whether vitamin C was responsible for the differences observed within the study. Both groups could have below-average acne and the argument's reasoning would be unchanged.
161	> D: If placebo subjects consumed foods naturally rich in vitamin C, this muddies the distinction between the two groups. The control group was effectively getting some vitamin C through diet, which could have reduced their acne. If the placebo group's acne was already being reduced by dietary vitamin C, then the absence of a difference between groups does not prove vitamin C is ineffective — it suggests both groups were receiving some vitamin C. However, this actually supports the argument's conclusion more than it weakens it: if both groups got vitamin C and there was no difference, then additional vitamin C through pills provides no extra benefit.
162	> E: Some subjects who knew they had placebos did not take them. This does not meaningfully affect the argument. Those subjects are in the placebo group regardless — they were not receiving vitamin C whether or not they swallowed their placebo pills. A placebo contains no active ingredient, so not taking it produces the same pharmacological result as taking it. At most, this introduces a minor methodological concern, but it does not provide any evidence that vitamin C actually reduces acne severity.

164	########## PT157-S2-Q19 | MBT/Most Supported (Must be True) | d5 | key C | bank Y ans C | 513w | score 4.0 | no-prediction; pt157-158-register
165	STIM: Committee chairperson: No new course will be approved for next year’s schedule unless a proposal for it has already been received either by this committee or by Dean Wilson. Dean Wilson has received only one new course proposal, and all of the new course proposals that this committee has received are for upper-level courses. It has been decided that, beginning next year, all upper-level courses will have prerequisites.
166	PROMPT: Which one of the following can be properly inferred if the chairperson’s statements are true?
167	  (A) If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then there will be no new courses next year that have prerequisites.
168	  (B) If all of the new courses next year are upper-level courses, then all of the new course proposals submitted to the committee will have been approved.
169	  (C*) If there is more than one new course next year, at least one of them will be a course that has a prerequisite.
170	  (D) If the new course proposal that Dean Wilson received is for an upper-level course, then all courses offered next year will have prerequisites.
171	  (E) If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then the new course proposal that was submitted to Dean Wilson will have been approved.
172	- [Given] No new course will be approved for next year's schedule unless a proposal for it has already been received either by this committee or by Dean Wilson. (NCA → PRC or PRDW) Dean Wilson has received only one new course proposal. (PRDW = 1) All of the new course proposals that this committee has received are for upper-level courses. (PRC → UL) Beginning next year, all upper-level courses will have prerequisites. (UL → P)
173	- [Evaluate] We can combine the final two conditional statements to conclude that all of the new course proposals that this committee has received will have prerequisites. (PRC → P)
174	> A: This answer says if there are no new upper-level courses, then no new courses will have prerequisites. We know upper-level courses have prerequisites (UL -> P), but this answer assumes the reverse: non-upper-level courses do not have prerequisites (~UL -> ~P). That is an illegal negation of the conditional. A lower-level course could also have prerequisites for reasons not discussed in the stimulus. The premises only tell us upper-level courses will have prerequisites, not that they are the only courses with prerequisites.
175	> B: This answer says if all new courses are upper-level, then all committee proposals were approved. We know the committee's proposals are for upper-level courses (PRC -> UL), but this answer assumes the reverse: upper-level courses came from the committee (UL -> PRC). That is an illegal reversal. Dean Wilson's proposal could also be for an upper-level course. Additionally, the committee could have received proposals without approving them. Nothing guarantees that receiving a proposal leads to approval.
176	> C*: If more than one new course is approved, at least two proposals were submitted. Dean Wilson received only one proposal, so he can account for at most one approved course. Any additional approved courses must have had proposals submitted to the committee. All committee proposals are for upper-level courses, and all upper-level courses have prerequisites. Therefore, at least one of the approved courses (the one that came through the committee) will have prerequisites. This follows directly from combining the premises without any unsupported inferences.
177	> D: This answer says if Dean Wilson's proposal is for an upper-level course, then all courses offered next year will have prerequisites. "All courses" goes far beyond what the premises support. The premises only tell us that new upper-level courses will have prerequisites — not that existing courses will have prerequisites. There could be many continuing lower-level courses on the schedule without prerequisites. This answer massively overgeneralizes from a rule about new upper-level courses to a claim about every course offered.
178	> E: This answer says if no new upper-level courses are approved, then Dean Wilson's proposal will have been approved. There is no logical connection between the absence of upper-level courses and Dean Wilson's proposal being approved. If no upper-level courses are approved, the committee proposals were rejected. But that tells us nothing about whether Dean Wilson's proposal was accepted or rejected — those are independent decisions. Dean Wilson's proposal could be rejected regardless of what happens with the committee's proposals.

181	STIM: Consumer advocate: Even relatively minor drug-related interactions can still be harmful to patients. For example, aspirin taken with fruit juice is ineffective. People unaware of this suffer unnecessary discomfort or take more aspirin than necessary. The government should, therefore, require drug companies to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions.
182	PROMPT: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the consumer advocate's argument?
183	  (A*) Providing information on minor drug-related interactions would detract from a patient’s attention to serious interactions.
184	  (B) Many drugs have fewer documented drug-related interactions than does aspirin.
185	  (C) Providing information about all drug-related interactions would result in only negligible price increases for consumers.
186	  (D) Current research is such that many drug-related interactions have not yet been identified.
187	  (E) Pharmacists usually draw patients’ attention to printed warnings that are provided with drugs.
188	- [Conclusion (Therefore)] The government should require drug companies to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions.
189	- [Evidence] Relatively minor drug-related interactions can still be harmful to patients (such as the fact that fruit juice can render aspirin ineffective, leading to people either not getting the pain relief they expected or to people taking more aspirin than necessary).
190	- [Evaluate] Given that ... relatively minor drug-related interactions can be harmful to patients (such as the fruit juice / aspirin example), How can we argue that ... drug companies should not be required to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions? The author is presenting one potential upside of this Plan / Recommendation (people taking aspirin would realize not to take it with fruit juice). We should try to think of potential downsides.
191	- [Goal] Look for an answer bringing up something negative about the plan of requiring drug companies to notify consumers of all known drug-related interactions. Is there some way in which it wouldn't be feasible or cost prohibitive? (Maybe it's impossible or impossibly costly to figure out every single way a new drug could interact with every other substance. Could it backfire in some way? (would people suddenly be afraid to take their needed medicines because they read about a possible interaction that is extremely rare but scares them out of taking their meds?)
192	> A*: This answer identifies the self-defeating consequence of the proposed policy. The consumer advocate wants all drug interactions disclosed so patients can avoid harmful interactions. But if the volume of information about minor interactions overwhelms patients, they will pay less attention to the serious, potentially life-threatening interactions that matter most. The policy would achieve the opposite of its intended purpose: instead of making patients safer, it would make them less safe by diluting their attention across a massive list of interactions, most of which are minor. The aspirin-and-fruit-juice type interactions would crowd out warnings about interactions that could cause organ damage, severe allergic reactions, or death. This is a classic "more is not always better" weakener — the proposed remedy creates a new problem that is worse than the original one.
193	> B: The fact that many drugs have fewer interactions than aspirin does not undermine the consumer advocate's argument. The advocate's proposal covers all drugs, not just aspirin. Even if most drugs have fewer interactions, the policy still requires disclosure of all known interactions for every drug. A drug with five interactions instead of fifty still requires those five to be disclosed under the proposed policy. The volume of information per drug is not the issue — the advocate's argument is about the principle of comprehensive disclosure regardless of quantity. This answer provides context about the aspirin example but does not challenge the reasoning or the policy recommendation in any meaningful way.
194	> C: If providing information about all interactions would result in only negligible additional costs to drug companies, this removes a potential practical objection to the policy, but it does not address the argument's logical vulnerability. The consumer advocate's argument could be weakened by showing the policy would be ineffective or counterproductive, not by showing it would be inexpensive. The cost of implementation is a practical concern, and showing that costs are negligible actually makes the policy look more feasible, not less desirable. This answer addresses a potential objection (cost) that the argument never raised, and it addresses it in a way that supports the policy rather than undermining it. The real weakness is about information overload, not financial burden.
195	> D: The fact that many interactions have not yet been identified is irrelevant to whether the government should require disclosure of those that have been identified. The advocate's proposal covers "all known" interactions, and the existence of unknown ones does not affect the case for disclosing known ones. If anything, this answer might slightly strengthen the advocate's position by suggesting the problem is even larger than currently understood — but that would only affect the scope of future disclosure, not the current proposal. The argument is about whether known interactions should be disclosed, not about the completeness of current research.
196	> E: If pharmacists draw patients' attention to printed warnings, this supports the idea that disclosure actually works — people do read and act on the information they receive. The consumer advocate argues that patients should be given interaction information, and this answer confirms that when warnings are provided, healthcare professionals help ensure patients notice them. Rather than weakening the argument, this answer provides evidence that the disclosure mechanism is effective: warnings are not ignored but are actively communicated by pharmacists. This makes the case for comprehensive disclosure stronger, since it suggests the information would actually reach and influence patients.

3	STIM: Mateo: Global warming has caused permafrost to melt under several arctic villages, forcing all their inhabitants to relocate at great expense. Since pollution from automobiles is a major contributor to global warming, the automotive industry should be required to help pay for the villagers' relocation.
4	PROMPT: Which one of the following principles, if valid, would most help to justify Mateo's argument?
5	  (A) Any industry has an obligation to pay for any damage that it should have known would result from its activities.
6	  (B) Manufacturers should be required to produce goods in a way that minimizes harm to people and the environment.
7	  (C) When the use of a product causes damage, governments should not be required to help pay for the damage unless those responsible for manufacturing the product are also required to help pay for them.
8	  (D*) Any industry manufacturing a product whose use contributes to costly damage for others should be liable for any damage generated by that product’s use.
9	  (E) An industry that contributes to global warming should be required to help pay for resulting damage to specific communities only if it has a general obligation to help pay for all damage produced by global warming.
10	- [Evidence (since)] Global warming has caused permafrost to melt under several arctic villages, forcing all their inhabitants to relocate at great expense.
11	Pollution from automobiles is a major contributor to global warming.
12	- [Conclusion (should)] The automotive industry should be required to help pay for the villagers' relocation.
13	- [Evaluate] The argument gives us evidence about automobiles contributing to a problem, then jumps to a conclusion claiming that the automobile industry should be required to help pay for that problem. This is an incredibly common pattern in Principle Strengthen questions. Regardless of whether or not that recommendation sounds reasonable, logically there is an important missing link in the argument.
14	For the argument to be valid, we need a premise to actually state that, if pollution from automobiles is a major cause of the village inhabitants needing to relocate, then the automotive industry should be required to help pay for the relocation.
15	```
16	autos are a cause → auto industry should pay
17	```
18	- [Goal] We're looking for an answer to make this missing connection. Since the correct answer only needs to strengthen the argument, it doesn't have to be a precise match for the assumption that we identified, but frequently it will be, so this a great question type to hone your prediction skills. Beware of answers that address a slightly different recommendation, make a recommendation for a different party, or reverse the logic. Eliminate any answer that has "auto industry should pay" as the sufficient condition.
19	> A: This principle requires that the damage be something the industry "should have known" would result from its product. But Mateo's argument never establishes — and does not need to establish — that the auto industry knew or should have foreseen this specific chain of consequences (pollution causes warming causes melting causes village relocation). Introducing a foreseeability or knowledge requirement creates a condition the argument's evidence does not satisfy. The auto industry could plausibly argue it did not foresee its products would melt permafrost beneath a distant village — and under this principle, they would escape liability. Mateo needs a strict causation-based principle with no escape hatches.
20	> B: Mateo's conclusion is specific: the auto industry should help PAY for the villagers' relocation costs. This principle establishes only a duty to "minimize harm," which is considerably weaker. "Minimize harm" could be satisfied by reducing emissions, investing in cleaner technology, or providing partial assistance — none of which necessarily includes paying full relocation costs. The principle must lead to the specific conclusion stated in the argument. A principle that leads to a vaguer or weaker conclusion does not fully justify the argument. The gap between "minimize harm" and "help pay relocation costs" is significant.
21	> C: This principle addresses government obligations, not industry liability. Mateo's conclusion is specifically that the automotive INDUSTRY should pay, not that the government should or shouldn't. Even if this principle established conditions under which the government should not foot the bill, that does not automatically shift the obligation to the auto industry. The argument needs a principle that directly creates a link between "your product caused damage" and "you should pay." This principle operates in a different sphere — government fiscal policy rather than industry liability — and cannot justify the specific conclusion about the auto industry's financial responsibility.
22	> D*: This principle states that any industry whose product's use contributes to costly damage should be liable for those costs. Apply it to Mateo's facts: (1) The auto industry manufactures cars. (2) The use of cars produces pollution that contributes to global warming. (3) Global warming causes permafrost to melt, forcing villagers to relocate at great expense. (4) Therefore, the auto industry's product contributes to costly damage. (5) By this principle, the auto industry should be liable for the relocation costs.
23	
24	The principle maps perfectly onto the argument. No extra conditions are required — no foreseeability, no intent, no knowledge. Just a clean causal connection between product use and damage, combined with the obligation to pay. The conclusion follows with logical certainty once this principle is accepted. This is exactly the kind of tight principle-to-conclusion alignment that Justify questions demand.
25	> E: This principle says the auto industry should be required to pay for THIS specific damage ONLY IF there is already a general obligation for it to pay for ALL damage its products cause. This is backwards from what Mateo needs. He wants to establish liability for one specific case, but this principle requires first proving universal liability — a much harder and broader task. It creates a precondition (general obligation to all damage) that must be satisfied before the specific obligation (pay for relocation) kicks in. Since Mateo has not established such a sweeping general obligation, this principle actually blocks his conclusion rather than supporting it.

27	########## PT158-S4-Q18 | Flaw (Flaw) | d5 | key B | bank Y ans B | 853w | score 8.0 | emdash-heavy; pt157-158-register; possible-misquote
28	STIM: Lindsey: Several people claim that our company was unfair when it failed to give bonuses to the staff. Perhaps they recalled that the company had promised that if it increased its profits over last year's, the staff would all get bonuses. However, the company's profit was much smaller this year than it was last year. Clearly, then, the company acted fairly.
29	PROMPT: The argument is flawed in that it
30	  (A) relies on the opinions of certain unnamed people without establishing that those people were well informed on the matter
31	  (B*) infers that an opinion is false merely because one potential reason for that opinion has been undermined
32	  (C) dismisses a claim on the basis of certain irrelevant attributes of the people who made the claim
33	  (D) confuses the size of a quantity with the amount by which that quantity has increased
34	  (E) overlooks the possibility that a policy can be fair even when it is not generous
35	- [Conclusion (clearly, then)] The company acted fairly in failing to give bonuses to the staff.
36	- [Evidence] Some people claim the company was unfair, perhaps because they recall that the company promised that if profits were higher than last year's, the staff would get bonuses.
37	But profits were much smaller than last year's.
38	- [Evaluate] Whenever we're doing Flaw and the author is rebutting some other position and concluding the polar opposite position, we want to be thinking about the famous Unproven vs. Proven False flaw.
39	EXAMPLE - Some people claim that cell phones cause cancer. But no scientific study has ever determined this. Thus, cell phones do not cause cancer.
40	The flaw here is that even though someone else failed to prove that cell phones DO cause cancer, that doesn't mean the author has presented any evidence that proves cell phones DON'T cause cancer.
41	In this argument, some people claim "the company acted unfairly" in failing to give the staff bonuses. Our author ends up concluding "the company acted fairly", and this is not by presenting positive evidence of her own in favor of this conclusion.
42	Instead, she's just taking down the argument that (she thinks) was being made in favor of the opposite conclusion.
43	- [Goal] The author should be responding to the actual reasons these several people offered in defense of their claim that the company was unfair, not speculating that "Perhaps they were basing their claim on X". So an answer might complain about that.
44	Even if the author is correct, and the people were basing their argument on X, the fact that the author has shown that X is not a valid way to prove "Unfair" doesn't suddenly mean that the author has proven "Fair". This is more in the Unproven vs. Proven False vein.
45	> A: This answer describes a flaw involving reliance on unnamed or poorly informed authorities. But Lindsey does not rely on unnamed sources to build her OWN case — she responds to other people's complaint by challenging their reasoning. The issue is not WHO is making the complaint or whether they are well-informed. The issue is HOW Lindsey responds to the complaint. She defeats one reason for the opinion and concludes the opinion is false. The flaw is structural (incomplete refutation), not epistemic (unreliable sources). Even if the complainers were perfectly informed experts, Lindsey's logical error would be identical.
46	> B*: This answer precisely identifies Lindsey's logical error. People claimed the company's decision was unfair, and one supporting reason was that the company had large profits (or profits had increased). Lindsey showed that this particular reason is factually weak — profits were actually smaller. So far, so good. But then she makes the leap: since this one reason is undermined, the entire opinion must be wrong, and the company acted fairly.
47	
48	This is a textbook incomplete refutation. An opinion can rest on multiple independent reasons. The employees might believe the decision was unfair because of contractual promises, precedent, management compensation, industry norms, or other factors Lindsey never addresses. By treating one challenged reason as the entire foundation for the opinion, Lindsey dramatically overstates what her evidence accomplishes. She refuted one premise and concluded she had refuted the conclusion — but conclusions can survive the loss of a single supporting reason if other reasons remain intact.
49	> C: This answer describes an ad hominem fallacy — dismissing a claim by attacking the character or attributes of the people making it rather than addressing the substance. But Lindsey does not attack the complainers personally. She does not question their intelligence, motives, credibility, or character. Instead, she directly engages with one factual claim they rely on (the profit level) and attempts to refute it. Lindsey's flaw is different: she engages with the substance but treats her rebuttal of one supporting reason as a complete refutation of the opinion. She deserves credit for engaging with the argument — she just does so in a logically incomplete way.
50	> D: This answer accuses Lindsey of confusing the size of profits with the amount by which profits increased — conflating absolute levels with rates of change. But Lindsey does not make this mathematical error. Her argument about profits is straightforward: people thought profits were large (or had increased), and Lindsey points out that profits were actually much smaller. She does not mix up "total profits" with "profit growth." Her error lies elsewhere — in the logical leap from "one reason for the opinion is wrong" to "the opinion itself is wrong." This answer identifies a mathematical confusion that is not present in the argument.
51	> E: This answer suggests Lindsey conflates fairness with generosity — perhaps arguing the company was fair when the real issue is that it was not generous. But Lindsey does not slide between these concepts. She directly argues the company's decision was fair, and the complainers directly argue it was unfair. Neither side introduces or confuses the concept of generosity. Lindsey's actual flaw is structural: she shows one reason for the unfairness claim is wrong and leaps to the conclusion that the company acted fairly. This is an incomplete refutation error, not a semantic confusion between "fair" and "generous." The flaw described in this answer simply is not present in Lindsey's reasoning.

Knowing that relational change sometimes fails to produce healing does not help establish that relational focus is necessary — if anything, it suggests that relational focus might not be sufficient, which is a different concern entirely. The argument's conclusion is that therapists must focus on relational change, not that relational change will definitely cure patients. Negate this answer: "some patients who change their relationships will not find relief."
