80	- [Evidence] Domesticated animals have come into existence by breeding only the tamest individuals of a wild species. If they we can't get our hands on multiple tame individuals, then we can't keep breeding tame-with-tame to get ever more tame versions of a hyena.

83	> A: The argument claims that wild animals can be domesticated through selective breeding. Whether domesticated animals can be turned back into wild ones through the reverse process is a completely separate question. The argument is about the feasibility of one-directional transformation — wild to domestic — and the reversibility of that process has no bearing on whether the forward process works for all species. Even if domesticated animals cannot be turned wild again, that tells us nothing about whether all wild species have the tame members needed to begin the domestication process. This answer addresses the reverse direction of a process when the argument is only about the forward direction.
84	> B: The frequency of mating between wild and tame members does not address the argument's core assumption. The argument claims all animals can be domesticated by selectively breeding tame individuals. Whether wild members mate more frequently than tame ones is a practical complication about breeding logistics, not a fundamental barrier to the process. As long as some tame individuals exist, a determined breeder could isolate them for selective breeding regardless of the mating preferences of wild members. The process might be slower or more difficult, but it would not be impossible in principle, and the argument's claim is about what is possible "in principle."

But this generalization assumes that every species has at least some tame individuals to work with. This answer directly attacks that assumption by stating that in some animal species, no members ever display tameness. If there are zero tame individuals, the selective breeding process cannot begin — there is nothing to select for. The method is useless without the raw material it requires. This eliminates the precondition for domestication in at least some species, directly contradicting the universal claim that "all animals" can be bred for domesticity.

A selective breeding program would take longer and be more difficult, but it could still proceed as long as tame individuals exist and can produce offspring. The argument's real vulnerability is the assumption that tame individuals exist in every species, not that the existing tame individuals breed at optimal rates. This answer identifies a speed bump, not a roadblock.

The fact that some dogs are tamer than others does not address whether, say, a completely wild species with no tame members can be domesticated. This answer describes a characteristic of the outcome of successful domestication rather than addressing whether the process can succeed universally. It is about post-domestication variation, while the argument's vulnerability concerns pre-domestication preconditions.

Maybe there is a ton of competition in the PC space (whereas there isn't for other high-tech items), since competition keeps prices lower.

103	> C: If PCs require more employee assistance than other tech items, retailers face higher labor costs per PC sale. Higher overhead means lower profit margins even with strong demand. Other tech items sold with less employee help have lower costs and thus higher margins.

It feels like the author is imposing some False Choice that if someone isn't a friend, they must be an enemy. 119	> A: This argument has a different logical structure from the stimulus. This is actually valid reasoning — if the requirements are genuinely necessary conditions, failing all of them does disqualify someone. The flaw in the stimulus was jumping from "fails friendship criteria" to "is an enemy" — a false dichotomy between two extreme categories. This answer concludes only that someone is not an officer, which is the direct logical consequence of failing the criteria. There is no leap to an extreme opposite category. It concludes "not-X" from failing the criteria for X, whereas the stimulus concluded "anti-X" from failing the criteria for X.
120	> B: This argument is logically valid, not flawed. It states that a plant needs sunny location AND regular watering to thrive. The plant is not in a sunny spot, so at least one necessary condition is unmet. The conclusion — that the plant will not thrive — follows logically. This is straightforward modus tollens: if both A and B are necessary for C, and A is absent, then C will not occur. The stimulus's flaw was a false dichotomy — leaping from "not friend" to "enemy." This answer contains no such leap; it simply concludes that a necessary condition is unmet, therefore the outcome will not occur. There is no jump to an opposite extreme category.
121	> C*: This answer perfectly mirrors the stimulus's flawed reasoning. The book has been widely reviewed and received no hostile reviews, so it does not meet the criteria for a "bad" book. The conclusion then leaps to: therefore it must be a masterpiece. This is the same false dichotomy as the stimulus. Just as the stimulus jumped from "not a friend" to "enemy" (ignoring mediocre, average, or indifferent relationships), this answer jumps from "not bad" (no hostile reviews) to "masterpiece" (extreme positive), ignoring the vast middle ground of books that are merely decent, competent, forgettable, or mediocre. The structure is identical: failing to meet the criteria for one extreme category, therefore belonging to the opposite extreme category, with all intermediate categories completely overlooked. A book with no hostile reviews could simply be unremarkable — just as someone who is not a friend could simply be a stranger.
122	> D: This argument says a decision to develop the northern border logically implies it is not in the town's best interest to use the land for recreation. This is a different type of flaw — it assumes that choosing one use for land automatically means an alternative use is not in the town's interest. The flaw here is about conflating a decision with a judgment about the best interest, or about opportunity cost and land use exclusivity. The stimulus's flaw is a false dichotomy between two extreme relational categories (friend/enemy). This answer does not leap from "not-X" to "opposite of X" — it draws an inference about competing uses of a resource. The logical structures are different: the stimulus treats two categories as exhaustive, while this answer treats two land uses as mutually exclusive and draws an evaluative conclusion about one from the selection of the other.
123	> E: This argument claims that if everyone belonged to certain intellectual professions, society would collapse. This is a conditional claim about a hypothetical universal scenario. The structure is entirely different from the stimulus. The stimulus identifies specific criteria not met by an individual and then classifies that individual in an extreme opposite category. This answer makes a prediction about a hypothetical world where everyone has the same profession. There is no "not-X therefore anti-X" structure, no false dichotomy between two extreme categories, and no ignored middle ground. The flaw, if any, is about the unrealistic hypothetical or about overlooking that society can have some authors without everyone being one. The logical pattern does not match.

195	STIM: Official: Six months ago, the fines for parking violations on the city's streets were raised to help pay for the parking garage that had just opened. Since then, parking violations on our streets have dropped by 50 percent. Hence, if we want there to be even fewer parking violations, the fines should be raised again.
196	PROMPT: The reasoning in the official's argument is flawed in that the argument
197	  (A) takes a possible effect of a reduction to be a possible cause of that reduction
198	  (B) takes for granted that raising fines a second time will reduce parking violations at least as much as it did the first time
199	  (C) fails to take into account the financial benefits the city is now deriving from fines for parking violations
200	  (D) takes for granted that people who park their cars illegally would prefer to park legally
201	  (E*) fails to establish that the initial decrease in parking violations was not due to the availability of additional parking spaces
202	- [Conclusion (Hence)] If we want there to be even fewer parking violations, the fines should be raised again.
203	- [Evidence] Six months ago we raised the fines for parking violations, and since then parking violations on our streets have dropped by 50%.
204	- [Evaluate] A common trope in Logical Reasoning is to say, "Policy change X went into effect. Then thing Y happened. So, apparently policy change X caused thing Y."
205	It seems here that our author is definitely making that same causal assumption, that the increase in fines is the cause of the 50% drop in parking violations.
206	The conclusion has implicit causality. When you say, "If you want X, you should do Y", you're implying that Y can bring about X.
207	This is the classic Explain Curious Fact argument structure, and when it shows up on Flaw, it means that they are usually just testing our awareness that the author has overconfidently arrived at one possible explanation for a curious fact, even though others could exist.
208	- [Goal] So we might see answers saying, "The author takes for granted that because X was followed by Y, X caused Y" or "the author fails to consider other things could have caused there to be 50% fewer parking violations (a pandemic or storm that led to there being fewer people out and about, or maybe an increase in visibility of meter maids making people worry about whether they still had value left on their meter).
209	But there's also a prediction aspect to the conclusion, so even if we thought the fines were the causal reason that violations dropped by 50%, we won't necessarily get a further reduction by increasing fines even more. At a certain point, you've already maxed out the disincentive to get a parking violation. Whether it's a $500 or $600 fine, I'm equally determined to not get that ticket.
210	> A: Confusing cause and effect means reversing the causal direction — claiming X caused Y when actually Y caused X. The argument's timeline is straightforward and correct: fines were raised, then violations decreased. There is no reversal of the causal arrow. The actual flaw is different: the argument ignores a confounding variable (the parking garage) that could explain the same outcome. This is an alternative-cause error, not a reversed-causation error. The argument gets the temporal sequence right but fails to consider that another simultaneous change might be the real explanation. These are distinct logical errors, and this answer identifies the wrong one.
211	> B: This answer identifies a real but secondary issue: diminishing returns. Perhaps the first fine increase captured the "easy wins" — the most price-sensitive violators — and a second increase would affect fewer people. But this critique is premature. The primary flaw is more fundamental: the argument has not established that fines caused the initial decrease at all. The parking garage is an unexamined alternative explanation. Before questioning whether a second dose of medicine will work as well as the first, we should first confirm the first dose was actually medicine and not a placebo. This answer skips ahead to a secondary objection while missing the foundational problem.
212	> C: This answer introduces a cost-benefit analysis: do the revenues from fines justify the costs of enforcement? But the argument's conclusion is about effectiveness ("raise fines to reduce violations further"), not about profitability ("raise fines to make money"). The logical flaw lies in the causal reasoning — attributing the decrease in violations to fines while ignoring the garage. Whether fines are financially worthwhile is a completely separate question from whether they actually caused the behavioral change. This answer operates in the wrong analytical framework for a flaw question about causal reasoning.
213	> D: This answer claims the flaw is assuming illegal parkers would prefer to park legally. But the argument's logic relies on deterrence through financial punishment, not on changing people's parking preferences. Deterrence works even when people prefer the illegal option — they choose the legal option to avoid the fine, not because they like it better. The argument does not need to assume anything about preferences; it only needs to assume fines change behavior through the threat of cost. The actual flaw is much simpler and more fundamental: the argument ignores that the new parking garage, not the fines, might explain the reduced violations.
214	> E*: This is the classic alternative-cause or confounding-variable flaw. Two events occurred simultaneously: the fine increase and the garage opening. Violations decreased by 50%. The argument attributes the decrease entirely to the fine increase and recommends a second increase. But it fails to consider that the parking garage — which provided new legal parking spaces — could be the actual explanation. People may have stopped parking illegally because they finally had a convenient legal option, not because they feared costlier tickets. Without controlling for this alternative cause, the argument's causal attribution is unjustified, and its recommendation (raise fines again) rests on an unproven foundation.

They found that the babies paid significantly more attention when the intervals were perfect octaves, fifths, or fourths than otherwise. These intervals are prevalent in the musical systems of most cultures around the world. Thus, humans probably have a biological predisposition to pay more attention to those intervals than to others. 
BANK: In an experiment, researchers played a series of musical intervals—pairs of notes that were either harmonious or dissonant. Each time, subjects indicated whether they found the notes pleasant or unpleasant. Most subjects found harmonious intervals pleasant and dissonant intervals unpleasant. However, when the same pairs of notes were played to members of an isolated tribe living in a remote area, these people showed no preference for harmonious notes over dissonant ones.

# LR explanations worth revamping

Source: the DB dump in `golden-survey/db/`: 3,419 explanations, `standard` where it exists, else `single`. Difficulty (1-5) comes from the question bank in `lsatu-wt-parser/public/data`; `?` means the bank has no entry for that question. The standard is [[golden-set]] and [[golden-set-observations]]. Every row with its score and flags: `inventory.csv` (columns `verdict`, `verdict_source`, `verdict_note` carry this list).

## Counts

| Severity | Items | Read in full | Listed by rule, not read |
|---|---|---|---|
| Revamp | 192 | 88 | 104 |
| Weak | 100 | 95 | 5 |
| Watch | 231 | 8 | 223 |
| **Total** | **523** | **191** | **332** |

- **Revamp**: fails as teaching or contains an error. Includes all 126 PT157/158 explicit-Standard rows. I read 25 of those; all 25 show the em-dash, padded register, and 9 also have a misquote, wrong logic, outside facts or a self-contradiction (8 of the 9 are PT158).
- **Weak**: complete or nearly so, but loose: role labels as notes ("This is a premise"), "not relevant" notes, no prediction. A student gets little from it.
- **Watch**: the teaching is sound but something mechanical is wrong: no breakdown, stray markup, LSAT Lab leftovers, a flattened table, a small slip.
- "Listed by rule" means the item was caught by an exact rule (PT157/158 explicit Standard, literal "- paragraph", zero breakdown steps, missing notes, a named LSAT Lab leftover) and not read. Everything else was read against the stimulus, the stem and the bank answer choices.

## How far to trust the scan

I read a random 30 from outside the top 150 (scores under 9.5). None needed a revamp. 6 were Weak, and all 6 scored 5 or more. 8 were Watch. 16 were fine. Every row scoring 6 or more was Weak or Watch. Of the 20 scoring under 3, 16 were fine and 4 had a small defect the scan missed (spaces lost around emphasis, "per capita" for "per calorie", a class-recording reference, stray markup). So the score ranks weakness well, over-rates "no breakdown" when the notes are good, and misses small transcription damage. The automatic misquote check was too noisy to use, because the house style quotes paraphrases; every misquote below was found by reading.

**Not yet read:** 136 more rows score between 7 and 9.5 and are not on this list. Going by the random check, most are Weak: voice-B notes that only name the role ("This is a premise"), concentrated in PT123-138. Sort `inventory.csv` by `score` to work through them.

Where the column is \"-\", the notes are longer but pasted or label-only.\\n')\nout.append('| Question | Type | Diff | Verdict-only notes |\\n|---|---|---|---|')\nfor v in sorted(vo, key=lambda x: key(x[0])):\n    out.append('| %s | %s | %s | %s |' % v)\nopen('choice-note-issues.md', 'w', encoding='utf-8').write('\\n'.join(out).replace('—', ','))\nprint({k: len(v) for k, v in groups.items()}, len(vo))\n"}

# Choice Note problems confirmed by reading

Each row was checked against the stimulus and the answer choices in the question bank (`lsatu-wt-parser/public/data`), since the DB rows do not store the choices. Companion to [[revamp-list]].

**Key conflicts:** none found. The scan flagged 16 notes that seemed to call a wrong letter correct; every one was a "Yes, the author assumes this, but..." opening. No explanation argues for a letter other than `correct_choice`. Two items point students the wrong way without contradicting the key: PT158-S4-Q22 (the breakdown predicts wrong choice D) and PT108-S3-Q6 (the notes belong to a different question; see side-issues).

**Popular traps:** the DB has no answer-choice percentages, and the local markdown that does was out of scope, so "verdict-only note on the popular trap" could not be tested directly. What can be said: 577 explanations have at least one wrong-answer note under 15 words or a bare "not relevant" / "out of scope" verdict (`verdict_only` column in `inventory.csv`), and in the 100 Weak items that is the typical note.

## Misquotes (the note describes words that are not in the choice) (9)

| Question | Type | Diff | Choice | Problem | Quote or detail |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| PT105-S4-Q14 | Necessary Assumption | 4 | D (correct) | misquote | "Choice (D) states that upper-income citizens could use the money"; D says "would use". |
| PT108-S3-Q6 | Paradox | 4 | all | wrong question | All five notes (predators, anglers, cooler water) answer a different question from the official one (algae increase; choices about detergents, temperature, oxygen). |
| PT137-S3-Q25 | Agree/Disagree | 2 | breakdown | wrong question | Evaluate: "Denise and Reshmi agree that people decide whether to commit a crime." |
| PT137-S4-Q15 | MBT/Most Supported | 4 | B | misquote | Note: "will worsen soil damage"; choice says "worsen soil drainage". |
| PT158-S2-Q22 | Parallel Flaw | 4 | D | misquote | Describes D as "not in the town's best interest to use the land for recreation"; D is about developing the other borders. |
| PT158-S3-Q2 | Strengthen | 1 | D | misquote | Note is about students who "can effectively combine knowledge"; D says they "can effectively solve many problems". (Also found in the 2026-09-26 survey.) |
| PT158-S3-Q9 | Weaken | 4 | C | misquote | Treats "negligible price increases for consumers" as "negligible additional costs to drug companies". |
| PT158-S3-Q13 | Necessary Assumption | 5 | E | misquote | Argues against "will not help patients address any difficulties they have in their relationships", not in E. |
| PT158-S3-Q17 | Principle-Conform | 5 | A, B, C, D | misquote | Notes describe choices that do not exist: "(2) enjoy learning math", "(2) have customers recommend the product", "(2) borrowers to return books in good condition", "(2) its employees to start working on Saturdays". |

## Wrong logic, wrong speakers, contradictions and outside facts (47)

| Question | Type | Diff | Choice | Problem | Quote or detail |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| PT112-S1-Q9 | Paradox | 4 | B (correct) | slip | "close to being 3 mg iron per capita" (per calorie); "raising" for raisins. |
| PT116-S2-Q8 | Strengthen | 3 | C | hedged logic | "And it almost might suggest to us that prison terms are currently too lax to be a deterrent, but raising them might work." |
| PT116-S3-Q6 | Agree/Disagree | 3 | C | wrong logic | "Speaker two definitely agrees"; Maria never says the stock market provides reliable income. |
| PT123-S3-Q7 | Agree/Disagree | 2 | A | wrong logic | "What is desirable is not discussed by neither Antonio nor Marla." Antonio says moderation costs "the joy of spontaneity". |
| PT124-S3-Q3 | Main Conclusion | 2 | D | mislabel | "This applies the opposing point..."; the point is the author's own concession. |
| PT127-S2-Q8 | Weaken | 4 | A | wrong logic | "Other boulders are not relevant to the argument." A is about how far glaciers carry boulders, which bears on "hundreds of miles". |
| PT127-S2-Q8 | Weaken | 4 | C | thin / pasted | Same note as B; never says a northern source is consistent with a southward glacier. |

Companion to [[revamp-list]] and [[choice-note-issues]]. Ordered by how much a student could be misled.\n\n## 1. Content that belongs to a different question\n\nThese show a student reasoning that does not match the question in front of them.\n\n| Question | What is wrong | Evidence |\n|---|---|---|\n| PT108-S3-Q6 | The DB stimulus and all five notes are for a different question from the official one. | DB stimulus: the bass catch downstream of the power plant fell. Bank stimulus for PT108-S3-Q6: the algae population downstream *rose*, with choices about detergents, water temperature and dissolved oxygen. The notes talk about predators, anglers and cooler water, which match neither set of choices. DB key C and bank key C agree only by coincidence. Needs a source check before anything else. |\n| PT137-S3-Q25 | Evaluate step is from another question. | \"Denise and Reshmi agree that people decide whether to commit a crime.\" The speakers are Graham and Adelaide (chess computer). |\n| PT143-S4-Q18 | Speaker table and Evaluate are from PT137-S3-Q25's template. | Table headed \"Graham / Adelaide\" (speakers are Wong and Tate); Evaluate says they disagree about countries being \"better off as democracies\" (the issue is autocracies). |\n| PT135-S4-Q25 | A note is from another question. | \"Neither Samuel nor Tova address a general trend of modern life.\" Speakers are Lutsina and Priscilla. |\n| PT133-S3-Q4 | E note carries speakers from PT142-S2-Q7. | \"Neither Talbert nor Sklar would agree with this statement.\" Speakers are Sam and Meli. |\n\n## 2. Suspected mis-keys\n\nNo explanation argues for a letter other than `correct_choice`. The scan's 16 \"key conflict\" hits in notes were all \"Yes, the author assumes this, but...\" openings. Two related items:\n\n- **PT158-S4-Q22** (Weaken, d5): the breakdown's Goal predicts the \"different epistemologists contradict each other\" objection, which is choice D. The key (E, \"much debate as to which thinkers were epistemologists\") is right, and the E note explains it well, but the breakdown steers students to D and the D note then calls contradiction \"a feature, not a bug\". Rewrite the breakdown.\n- **Bank answer keys disagree with the DB on 18 questions**, all of them bank entries filed under a different PT, section or question number (\"moved\" in `inventory.csv`). I read five of these (PT116-S2-Q6, Q7, Q8; PT131-S3-Q16, Q17) and the DB key is correct in every case. So the bank's `correctAnswer` is wrong for these, not the DB. Anything that reads the bank's key (drills, analytics, the golden-survey inventory) would mis-grade them:\n\n  PT101-S2-Q19 (DB E, bank D), PT101-S3-Q19 (D/E), PT113-S3-Q21 (C/A), PT113-S3-Q22 (D/A), PT113-S3-Q23 (B/A), PT113-S3-Q24 (E/A), PT116-S2-Q4 (B/C), PT116-S2-Q6 (E/B), PT116-S2-Q7 (C/D), PT116-S2-Q8 (C/E), PT119-S2-Q19 (B/E), PT119-S2-Q21 (A/C), PT119-S4-Q8 (A/C), PT129-S3-Q18 (B/D), PT129-S3-Q19 (D/A), PT131-S3-Q16 (E/C), PT131-S3-Q17 (A/E), PT148-S4-Q24 (E/A).\n\n  Note PT113-S3-Q21 to Q24 all have bank key A, which looks like a placeholder.\n\n## 3. Bank alignment\n\n- **PT119-S2-Q19** is matched to bank entry PT103-S2-Q24, which is a different question on the same topic (musical intervals; the bank one is about an isolated tribe's preferences). Its difficulty and features in `inventory.csv` are borrowed from the wrong question.\n- **PT101-S3-Q1**: the bank stimulus differs from the DB's in both speakers' text (bank: election boards hiring extra help; Sharon concludes \"apathy\"). The DB text reads like the real question. The bank's choices for this question may not match either.\n- 110 explained questions have no bank entry at all, so there is no difficulty and no answer-choice text on disk to check notes against (for example PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3, PT131-S2-Q5).\n\n## 4. Empty or partial explanations\n\n| Problem | Count | Questions |\n|---|---|---|\n| Explanation row exists with no steps and no notes | 2 | PT122-S1-Q9, PT147-S4-Q6 |\n| Breakdown only, no choice notes | 16 | PT102-S3-Q14, PT117-S3-Q2, PT117-S3-Q8, PT117-S4-Q3, PT117-S4-Q5, PT118-S1-Q9, PT118-S3-Q8, PT119-S2-Q7, PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S2-Q24, PT119-S3-Q3, PT119-S3-Q20, PT119-S4-Q2, PT120-S1-Q23, PT120-S1-Q26, PT120-S4-Q17 |\n| Some notes missing | 8 | PT105-S4-Q14 (3 of 5), PT133-S2-Q25 (3, including the correct answer), PT107-S3-Q7, PT108-S3-Q16, PT111-S1-Q22, PT119-S2-Q15, PT146-S3-Q11, PT156-S2-Q19 (4 each) |\n| Placeholder text as a note | 2 | PT147-S4-Q4 A (correct), PT147-S4-Q3 D (correct): \"(No authored tag or explanation for this choice.)\" |\n| Notes but no Argument Breakdown | 224 | Heaviest in PT148 (33), PT111 (14), PT122 (11), PT116 (10), PT102 (9). |\n| Question has no explanation at all | 352 | Known (`is_explained = false`); the 772-gap plan covers these. |\n\nSeveral breakdown-only rows (PT117-S3-Q8, PT118-S1-Q9, PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3, PT119-S2-Q24, PT119-S4-Q2, PT120-S1-Q23, PT120-S4-Q17) are written in a different register from the LSAT Lab text: bracketed labels like \"Rossi's argument structure\", em-dashes, \"i.e.\" glosses. They look like later fill-ins written without the answer choices. Two of them (PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3) have no bank entry, so the choices may never have been available to whoever wrote them.\n\n## 5. LSAT Lab leftovers\n\nText that refers to things students on LSAT U cannot see:\n\n- \"try this one\" with no link: PT102-S2-Q20, PT139-S1-Q5, PT140-S3-Q18, PT158-S4-Q25.\n- \"click on the bold if you'd like to see other examples\": PT140-S3-Q7.\n- \"watch the class recording, via the play button on the highlighter toolbar\": PT132-S4-Q18.\n- \"Welcome to Test 89, everyone\": PT155-S4-Q15 (LSAT Lab's numbering).\n- \"the sentence ends here with no continuation on the LSAT Lab page\": PT143-S4-Q9 (a transcription note left in student text).\n- \"As we analyzed\" with no analysis present (the breakdown is missing): PT148-S3-Q19 A, B, E.\n\n## 7. Question-type labels\n\n`lr_questions.question_type` has **82 distinct values** for about 18 real types: the same type appears as \"Must be True\", \"Must Be True\", \"Must Be True (Inference)\", \"Must be True (EXCEPT)\"; \"Paradox EXCEPT\" and \"Paradox (EXCEPT)\"; 30-odd \"(type inferred)\" suffixes. Anything that groups by this column (drill filters, analytics) will split types.\n\nLabels that are plainly wrong for the stem:\n\n| Question | Label | Stem says |\n|---|---|---|\n| PT108-S2-Q13 | Strengthen | \"most strongly supports the claim that Ramona and Martin agree\" (Agree/Disagree) |\n| PT108-S3-Q20 | Strengthen | \"provide the most support for holding that they disagree\" (Agree/Disagree) |\n| PT109-S4-Q14 | Strengthen | \"The statements above, if true, most strongly support which one of the following\" (Most Supported) |\n| PT125-S4-Q12 | Parallel Flaw | \"which one of the following is most supported by them\" (Most Supported) |\n| PT125-S4-Q16 | Most Supported | \"most vulnerable to criticism\" (Flaw) |\n| PT113-S4-Q12, PT128-S2-Q3 | Necessary Assumption | \"is flawed because...\" (Flaw) |\n| PT125-S4-Q14, PT128-S3-Q12, PT151-S2-Q1, PT151-S3-Q18 | Weaken | \"vulnerable to criticism / flawed because\" (Flaw) |\n| PT107-S3-Q6 | Flaw | \"a questionable assumption on which the argument relies\" (Necessary Assumption) |\n| PT132-S2-Q1 | Most Supported | \"principle best illustrated\" (Principle-Conform) |\n\n## 8. Smaller slips spotted while reading\n\n- PT126-S1-Q2 \"This fail to connect\"; PT127-S2-Q8 \"bolder\"; PT127-S3-Q19 \"Eric\" for Erich; PT148-S3-Q19 \"Panjak\"; PT131-S2-Q5 \"synthensized alphahyrdoxy\"; PT124-S1-Q12 \"lessons\" for lessens; PT135-S4-Q6 copies the stimulus as \"over- nitrified\" (it says overnutrified); PT112-S1-Q9 \"raising\" for raisins and \"per capita\" for per calorie; PT107-S3-Q6 \"is every badly weakened\".\n- The question bank carries a \"Games-y LR\" feature tag (already noted 2026-09-26); worth renaming given the no-Logic-Games rule for student copy.\n"}
